     Step 5: camelCase validation keys + GET /api/tasks (filter & sort)

     Context

     Step 4 is committed (73775a9): POST and GET-by-id, EnumNameAttribute, and
     ValidationProblemDetails with
     PascalCase error keys (Title), while the JSON body uses camelCase (title).
     Step 5 does two things:
     (a) it switches the error keys to camelCase so the UI can map errors
     straight onto fields, and (b) it adds
     GET /api/tasks with optional status and priority filters and an explicit
     priority-rank sort.

     Part A: camelCase validation error keys

     How: add one line in AddApiControllers()
     (Infrastructure/ServiceCollectionExtensions.cs):
     options.ModelMetadataDetailsProviders.Add(new
     SystemTextJsonValidationMetadataProvider());
     This built-in provider (in ASP.NET Core since 7.0) names ModelState entries
     with the same rules System.Text.Json
     uses: the camelCase naming policy, or [JsonPropertyName] if present. Keys
     become title and priority, matching
     the body. JSON parse errors already use $.priority, so they're unaffected.
     Messages stay as written
     ("Title is required.", "Priority must be one of: …"), because they come
     from explicit messages or the property's display name.

     Test changes:
     - ApiAssert.ValidationErrorAsync switches from the case-insensitive 
       substring match to an exact key match.
       The old match would have passed with either casing, so it couldn't catch
       a regression.
     - CreateTaskTests gets new keys: "Title"→"title", "Status"→"status",
       "Priority"→"priority",
       "Description"→"description", "AssignedTo"→"assignedTo", malformed JSON
       "$"→"$.title", numeric "$.priority".
       The GetProperty("Title") lookups change to "title" and so on.
     - New test Create_ValidationErrorKeys_AreCamelCase: posting { "title": "", 
       "priority": "Urgent", "assignedTo": <101 chars> }
       returns exactly the keys title, priority and assignedTo, and no
       PascalCase key.

     Part B: GET /api/tasks

     Request: GET /api/tasks?status=InProgress&priority=High. Both filters are
     optional.
     - Contracts/TaskListQuery.cs: a class with [FromQuery(Name = "status")] 
       [EnumName(typeof(TaskItemStatus))] string? Status
       and the same for priority. It's bound with [FromQuery] TaskListQuery 
       query, so the error keys are status and priority.
     - An empty value (?status=) binds to null and means "no filter". MVC
       converts empty strings to null by default.
     - If a parameter is repeated, the first value wins. Unknown query
       parameters are ignored.

     LINQ (Services/TaskQueries.cs, a static class, so it can be unit-tested and
     its SQL shown):
     /// Priority rank used for sorting. Enums are stored as strings, so ORDER 
     BY Priority would be alphabetical
     /// (Critical, High, Low, Medium). EF translates this to a SQL CASE 
     expression.
     public static readonly Expression<Func<TaskItem, int>> PriorityRank = t =>
         t.Priority == TaskPriority.Critical ? 4 :
         t.Priority == TaskPriority.High     ? 3 :
         t.Priority == TaskPriority.Medium   ? 2 : 1;

     public static IQueryable<TaskItem> FilterAndSort(this IQueryable<TaskItem> 
     tasks, TaskItemStatus? status, TaskPriority? priority)
     {
         if (status is not null)   tasks = tasks.Where(t => t.Status == status);
          // soft-deleted rows already excluded by the global filter
         if (priority is not null) tasks = tasks.Where(t => t.Priority ==
     priority);
         return tasks
             .OrderByDescending(PriorityRank)          // Critical, High, 
     Medium, Low
             .ThenByDescending(t => t.CreatedDate)     // newest first within a 
     priority
             .ThenBy(t => t.Id);                       // deterministic 
     tie-break
     }
     - TaskService.ListAsync(TaskListQuery, ct) parses the validated strings
       with EnumNameAttribute.Parse, then
       runs _db.Tasks.AsNoTracking().FilterAndSort(...).ToListAsync() and maps
       the results with ToResponse().
     - It logs Listed {Count} tasks (status {Status}, priority {Priority}) at
       Debug level, so routine reads stay out of Information.
     - TasksController.List([FromQuery] TaskListQuery query, ct) returns 200
       with TaskResponse[].

     Decisions (all confirmed by you)

     1. Invalid filter → 400 ValidationProblemDetails via the same [EnumName]
        attribute, e.g.
        errors.status: ["Status must be one of: Todo, InProgress, Done."].
        ?priority=2 is rejected the same way.
        A typed-enum query parameter would instead give "The value 'Urgent' is
        not valid for Status." without the allowed list.
     2. Case-insensitive filters (?status=inprogress). This matches how create
        accepts enum values.
     3. Empty result → 200 [], a bare JSON array, the same element shape as GET
        by id.
     4. No paging for now. The dataset is about 15 rows and the UI shows
        everything. Adding paging later means
        either an envelope (a breaking change to the response shape) or optional
        page/pageSize plus an
        X-Total-Count header (non-breaking). I'd go with the header approach
        when it's needed. I'm not adding a
        silent row cap, because that would hide data.

     Tests

     - Integration Integration/Tasks/ListTaskTests.cs (Database collection; each
       test inserts its own rows):
       - no filter returns all active tasks
       - status only, priority only, and both together
       - case-insensitive values
       - no match → 200 []
       - soft-deleted rows that match the filter are still excluded
       - sort order: four tasks inserted as Low, Critical, Medium, High →
         Critical, High, Medium, Low.
         This is the alphabetical-regression check, because alphabetical order
         would be Critical, High, Low, Medium.
         Ties within a priority come back newest CreatedDate first.
       - ?status= → treated as no filter
       - invalid status / priority → 400 with exact keys and the allowed-values
         message
       - ?priority=2 → 400
       - both invalid → both keys
     - Unit Unit/TaskQueriesTests.cs:
       - FilterAndSort on an in-memory IQueryable (LINQ-to-objects) checks the
         order and filters without a DB
       - ToQueryString() on a SQL Server context, with no connection needed,
         asserts the SQL contains a CASE in ORDER BY
         and no ORDER BY [t].[Priority]. This proves the sort runs in the
         database and not alphabetically.
     - Part A changes as listed above.

     Files

     - New:
       - Contracts/TaskListQuery.cs
       - Services/TaskQueries.cs
       - tests Integration/Tasks/ListTaskTests.cs and Unit/TaskQueriesTests.cs
     - Edited:
       - Infrastructure/ServiceCollectionExtensions.cs (camelCase provider)
       - Services/ITaskService.cs, Services/TaskService.cs,
         Controllers/TasksController.cs
       - tests/.../ApiAssert.cs, tests/.../CreateTaskTests.cs
       - TaskManager.Api.http, README.md

     Verification

     dotnet build
     dotnet test
     dotnet run --project src/TaskManager.Api --launch-profile http
     Against the seeded TaskManagerDb:
     - GET /api/tasks → 12 tasks: ids 4, 8, 12 (Critical) first, newest first
       within each priority
     - ?status=Done → 4
     - ?priority=critical → 3
     - ?status=Todo&priority=High → 1 (id 3)
     - ?status=Blocked → 400 errors.status
     - POST {"title":""} → 400 with the keys title and priority

      51 +    public async Task<IReadOnlyList<TaskResponse>> ListAsync(Ta
         +skListQuery query, CancellationToken cancellationToken)        
      52 +    {                                                          
      53 +        ArgumentNullException.ThrowIfNull(query);

      55 +        TaskItemStatus? status = query.Status is null ? null : 
         +EnumNameAttribute.Parse<TaskItemStatus>(query.Status);         
      56 +        TaskPriority? priority = query.Priority is null ? null

      58 +        var tasks = await _db.Tasks

      68      public async Task<TaskResponse> GetByIdAsync(int id, Cancel
          lationToken cancellationToken)

● Step 5 is done: dotnet test passes all 119 tests (21 new for listing). I
  haven't committed anything or started step 6. I didn't run the list requests
  by hand against TaskManagerDb; the expected results below come from the seed
  data.

  The list query SQL

  This is what ToQueryString() produces for ?status=InProgress&priority=High.
  The unit test FilterAndSort_TranslatesPrioritySortToSqlCase writes it to its
  output.
  DECLARE @__wantedStatus_0 nvarchar(20) = N'InProgress';
  DECLARE @__wantedPriority_1 nvarchar(20) = N'High';

  SELECT [t].[Id], [t].[AssignedTo], [t].[CreatedDate], [t].[Description],
  [t].[IsDeleted],
         [t].[ModifiedDate], [t].[Priority], [t].[RowVersion], [t].[Status],
  [t].[Title]
  FROM [Tasks] AS [t]
  WHERE [t].[IsDeleted] = CAST(0 AS bit) AND [t].[Status] = @__wantedStatus_0
  AND [t].[Priority] = @__wantedPriority_1
  ORDER BY CASE
      WHEN [t].[Priority] = N'Critical' THEN 4
      WHEN [t].[Priority] = N'High' THEN 3
      WHEN [t].[Priority] = N'Medium' THEN 2
      ELSE 1
  END DESC, [t].[CreatedDate] DESC, [t].[Id]
  - Soft delete: IsDeleted = 0 comes from the global query filter, so the
    service never has to add it.
  - Filters: they're sent as typed nvarchar(20) parameters, never pasted into
    the SQL text.
  - Sorting: priority is sorted by a CASE rank in SQL Server, not
    alphabetically. The test also checks there's no ORDER BY [t].[Priority].

  The LINQ that produces it is in src/TaskManager.Api/Services/TaskQueries.cs.
  It applies the optional status and priority filters, then sorts by
  PriorityRank (descending), then newest CreatedDate, then Id.

  How to verify

  dotnet build
  dotnet test
  dotnet run --project src/TaskManager.Api --launch-profile http
  Then use the new "list" section in src/TaskManager.Api/TaskManager.Api.http,
  or curl.exe. Expected results against the seeded TaskManagerDb:

  Request: GET /api/tasks
  Expected: 12 tasks, in id order 12, 8, 4, 11, 7, 3, 10, 6, 2, 9, 5, 1
    (Critical, High, Medium, Low; newest first within each)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request: GET /api/tasks?status=Done
  Expected: 4 tasks: 12, 11, 10, 9
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request: GET /api/tasks?priority=critical
  Expected: 3 tasks: 12, 8, 4
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request: GET /api/tasks?status=Todo&priority=High
  Expected: 1 task: id 3
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request: GET /api/tasks?status=InProgress&priority=High
  Expected: only id 7, because seed id 14 matches but is deleted
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request: GET /api/tasks?status=
  Expected: all 12 (an empty value means no filter)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request: GET /api/tasks?status=Blocked&priority=Urgent
  Expected: 400, errors.status and errors.priority with the allowed values
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request: GET /api/tasks?priority=2
  Expected: 400, errors.priority

  For example: curl.exe -s 
  "http://localhost:5157/api/tasks?status=Todo&priority=High"

  Things you should know

  - Plan correction: the plan said the list would start "ids 4, 8, 12". The
    correct order is 12, 8, 4, because newest comes first within a priority. The
    table above is right.
  - Build fix: the first build failed because I named a test helper Task(...),
    which clashes with .NET's Task type. I renamed it to NewTask(...) in both
    new test files.

● Approved Claude's plan
  ⎿  Plan saved to: 
     ~\.claude\plans\pasted-content-id-0656-i-m-working-expressive-umbrella.md ·
     /plan to edit
     Step 6: Update (PUT full edit + PATCH status)

     Context

     Step 5 is committed (defecbf): list, create and get, camelCase validation
     keys, EnumNameAttribute, and
     TaskResponse.rowVersion as base64. GlobalExceptionHandler already maps
     DbUpdateConcurrencyException → 409.
     Step 6 adds PUT /api/tasks/{id} for the edit form, using optimistic
     concurrency, and
     PATCH /api/tasks/{id}/status for the status dropdown, which is
     last-write-wins.

     Request DTOs (Contracts/)

     - TaskFieldsRequest (abstract base) moves the shared, already-validated
       fields out of CreateTaskRequest:
       title, description, priority and assignedTo, with the same attributes and
       length constants.
       CreateTaskRequest inherits it and keeps status optional. Its behaviour
       doesn't change, and the existing tests prove that.
     - UpdateTaskRequest : TaskFieldsRequest, plus:
       - [Required] [EnumName] status: required on PUT, because a full edit
         sends every field
       - [Required(ErrorMessage = "RowVersion is required.")] [RowVersion] 
         string? rowVersion
     - UpdateTaskStatusRequest: [Required] [EnumName(typeof(TaskItemStatus))] 
       string? status, and nothing else.
     - Fields PUT can change: title, description, status, priority, assignedTo.
       It can't change: id (taken from the
       route), createdDate, modifiedDate (set by the server) or isDeleted. Those
       aren't on the DTO, so if a client sends them
       they're silently ignored, the same as over-posting on create.

     rowVersion in and out

     - Out: unchanged. byte[] is serialized as base64 by System.Text.Json, e.g.
       "AAAAAAAAB9E=".
     - In: a string validated by a new Validation/RowVersionAttribute. It
       accepts null ([Required] handles that),
       and the value must pass Convert.TryFromBase64String and decode to exactly
       8 bytes (SQL rowversion).
       - Missing → 400 errors.rowVersion: ["RowVersion is required."]
       - Malformed or wrong length → 400 errors.rowVersion: ["RowVersion must be
         the base64 value returned by the API."]
       - These are the same ValidationProblemDetails shape and camelCase key as
         create.
       - A byte[] DTO property would instead fail inside the JSON parser, as
         $.rowVersion / "The input was not valid.".
     - How the check works (EF): load the tracked entity, then
       db.Entry(task).Property(t => t.RowVersion).OriginalValue = clientBytes;.
       EF puts the client's version in
       UPDATE … WHERE Id = @id AND RowVersion = @original. Zero rows updated →
       DbUpdateConcurrencyException →
       the existing handler returns 409 "The task was changed by someone else.
       Reload it and try again."
       - One mechanism covers both cases: a version that was stale before the
         request, and a write that sneaks in
         between load and save.
       - A PUT always sets ModifiedDate, so an UPDATE is always issued and the
         check always runs, even when the other values are identical.

     Service (ITaskService / TaskService)

     - UpdateAsync(int id, UpdateTaskRequest, ct):
       a. Find the task through the global filter, or throw NotFoundException,
          so unknown and soft-deleted ids are both 404.
       b. Set OriginalValue(RowVersion).
       c. Assign the five fields (trimmed, blank → null, like create) and
          ModifiedDate = timeProvider.GetUtcNow().
          CreatedDate is never touched.
       d. Save, log Updated task {TaskId}, and return the TaskResponse with the
          new rowVersion.
     - UpdateStatusAsync(int id, UpdateTaskStatusRequest, ct): find it (or 404),
       set the status and ModifiedDate, save,
       and log Changed status of task {TaskId} from {OldStatus} to {NewStatus}.
       It returns the updated task with the new rowVersion.
       - The client sends no rowVersion, so this is last-write-wins.
       - The only conflict left is a write that lands in the milliseconds
         between load and save. That gives a 409, which is correct and very
         rare.

     Controller

     - [HttpPut("{id:int:min(1)}")] Update(int id, [FromBody] UpdateTaskRequest,
       ct) returns 200 TaskResponse
     - [HttpPatch("{id:int:min(1)}/status")] UpdateStatus(int id, [FromBody] 
       UpdateTaskStatusRequest, ct) returns 200 TaskResponse
     - Both use application/json and [ProducesResponseType] for 200, 400, 404
       (and 409 on PUT).
     - 200 with a body, rather than 204, so the UI gets the new rowVersion and
       ModifiedDate.
     - CORS already allows PUT and PATCH.

     Decisions (all confirmed by you)

     1. rowVersion input is a string with [RowVersion], giving a 400 under
        rowVersion (see above).
     2. PATCH is last-write-wins, takes no rowVersion, and returns the fresh
        rowVersion (as you suggested).
     3. PUT is a true replacement. An omitted description or assignedTo is
        cleared to null. The UI's edit form
        always sends every field, so this is safe.
     4. PATCH with the same status is a no-op: 200 with the task unchanged. No
        write, so ModifiedDate and rowVersion stay
        the same. PUT always writes, because that's how the concurrency check
        runs.
     - Fixed by your brief: ModifiedDate = TimeProvider now on every write,
       CreatedDate never changes,
       and unknown or soft-deleted ids return 404 on both endpoints (validation
       runs first, so a bad body on an unknown id is 400).

     Tests

     - Unit (Unit/TaskServiceTests.cs, EF InMemory + FakeTimeProvider):
       - UpdateAsync_SetsModifiedDateToNow_AndKeepsCreatedDate: create at T0,
         advance the fake clock 2h, update, and assert
         Modified = T0+2h, Created = T0, and the fields changed
       - UpdateStatusAsync_SetsModifiedDate_AndKeepsOtherFields
       - UpdateStatusAsync_SameStatus_DoesNotTouchModifiedDate
       - UpdateAsync_UnknownOrDeleted_ThrowsNotFound
       - InMemory checks concurrency tokens, so
         UpdateAsync_StaleRowVersion_ThrowsConcurrency is included if it behaves
         the same
         there. The real 409 check is in the integration tests either way.
     - Unit (Unit/RowVersionAttributeTests.cs): valid 8 bytes; null accepted;
       "", "not-base64!!", "AAAA" (3 bytes) and 16 bytes rejected.
     - Integration (Integration/Tasks/UpdateTaskTests.cs, PUT):
       - 200 with every field changed, a new rowVersion, CreatedDate unchanged,
         ModifiedDate later than before, and GET reflects it
       - omitted description is cleared
       - over-posted id/createdDate/isDeleted are ignored
       - stale rowVersion: PUT A succeeds, then PUT B with the old version → 409
         ProblemDetails, and the row still has A's values
       - missing rowVersion → 400 rowVersion; malformed → 400; wrong length →
         400
       - invalid status or priority → 400 with the allowed list; missing status
         → 400; blank title → 400
       - unknown id → 404; soft-deleted id → 404; /api/tasks/0 → 404
     - Integration (Integration/Tasks/UpdateTaskStatusTests.cs, PATCH):
       - 200 with the new status, a fresh rowVersion that differs from before,
         ModifiedDate moved, CreatedDate and the other fields unchanged
       - two PATCHes in a row both succeed (last-write-wins)
       - case-insensitive values
       - same status → no-op
       - PATCH then PUT with the pre-PATCH rowVersion → 409, and with the
         PATCH's returned rowVersion → 200
         (this documents why the UI must refresh its copy)
       - invalid or missing status → 400 status
       - unknown or soft-deleted → 404

     Files

     - New:
       - Contracts/TaskFieldsRequest.cs, Contracts/UpdateTaskRequest.cs,
         Contracts/UpdateTaskStatusRequest.cs
       - Validation/RowVersionAttribute.cs
       - tests Unit/RowVersionAttributeTests.cs and
         Integration/Tasks/{UpdateTaskTests,UpdateTaskStatusTests}.cs
     - Edited:
       - Contracts/CreateTaskRequest.cs (inherits the base)
       - Services/ITaskService.cs, Services/TaskService.cs,
         Controllers/TasksController.cs
       - Unit/TaskServiceTests.cs
       - TaskManager.Api.http, README.md

     Verification

     dotnet build
     dotnet test
     dotnet run --project src/TaskManager.Api --launch-profile http
     Then, in the .http file:
     - GET /api/tasks/1 and copy rowVersion
     - PUT /api/tasks/1 with it → 200 and a new rowVersion
     - repeat the same PUT with the old rowVersion → 409
     - PATCH /api/tasks/1/status {"status":"Done"} → 200 and a new rowVersion
     - PUT with "rowVersion":"bad" → 400 errors.rowVersion
     - PUT /api/tasks/13 (soft-deleted) → 404

● Plan approved. First the request DTOs and the rowVersion validator.

● Write(src\TaskManager.Api\Contracts\CreateTaskRequest.cs)

  Here's UpdateAsync as written in src/TaskManager.Api/Services/TaskService.cs:

  /// <inheritdoc />
  public async Task<TaskResponse> UpdateAsync(int id, UpdateTaskRequest request,
  CancellationToken cancellationToken)
  {
      ArgumentNullException.ThrowIfNull(request);

      var task = await FindTrackedAsync(id, cancellationToken);

      // Optimistic concurrency: EF compares the ORIGINAL value of the 
  concurrency token in the
      // UPDATE's WHERE clause. Setting it to the client's rowVersion means the 
  update only succeeds
      // if nobody has changed the row since the client read it; otherwise 
  SaveChanges throws
      // DbUpdateConcurrencyException, which GlobalExceptionHandler maps to 409.
      _db.Entry(task).Property(t => t.RowVersion).OriginalValue =
  RowVersionAttribute.Decode(request.RowVersion!);

      task.Title = request.Title!.Trim();
      task.Description = NullIfBlank(request.Description);
      task.Status = EnumNameAttribute.Parse<TaskItemStatus>(request.Status!);
      task.Priority = EnumNameAttribute.Parse<TaskPriority>(request.Priority!);
      task.AssignedTo = NullIfBlank(request.AssignedTo);
      task.ModifiedDate = _timeProvider.GetUtcNow(); // always changes, so an 
  UPDATE (and the concurrency check) always runs
      // CreatedDate, IsDeleted and Id are never touched.

      await _db.SaveChangesAsync(cancellationToken);

      _logger.LogInformation("Updated task {TaskId}", task.Id);
      return task.ToResponse();
  }

  // Tracked load through the global query filter: unknown and soft-deleted ids 
  are both "not found".
  private async Task<TaskItem> FindTrackedAsync(int id, CancellationToken 
  cancellationToken) =>
      await _db.Tasks.FirstOrDefaultAsync(t => t.Id == id, cancellationToken)
          ?? throw new NotFoundException(ResourceName, id);

  About the OriginalValue line: it sets the original value, not the current one.
  EF uses the original value in UPDATE … WHERE [Id] = @id AND [RowVersion] = 
  @original. A stale client version therefore matches zero rows and EF throws
  DbUpdateConcurrencyException. That also catches someone else saving between
  our load and our save. Setting the current value instead would do nothing,
  because SQL Server generates rowversion itself.

    FakeTimeProvider in TaskServiceTests, and UpdateTaskTests for the
    integration side.
  - UpdateTaskStatusRequest.cs is also written, but it isn't wired up. PATCH
    waits until you've seen the PUT results.

  What these tests show:
  - Stale rowVersion gives a 409 with no data loss. A first PUT succeeds. A
    second PUT with the original rowVersion returns 409 with "The task was
    changed by someone else. Reload it and try again.", and the row still holds
    the first writer's values.
  - Dates behave as planned. Using the fake clock, the unit test advances 2
    hours: ModifiedDate becomes now + 2h and CreatedDate doesn't change. The
    integration test checks the same against the real database.
  - Bad rowVersions are rejected. A missing value returns "RowVersion is
    required.". Malformed values and wrong lengths (3 or 16 bytes) return
    "RowVersion must be the base64 value returned by the API.". Both are a 400
    under the rowVersion key. An empty string gets only the "required" message,
    not both.
  - The EF in-memory database also enforces the rowVersion. I'd only planned the
    stale-rowVersion unit test if it worked there, and it does, so it's
    included.

      110  A soft-deleted task is indistinguishable from one that never e
           xisted: both return 404.
      111 
      112 +**Update (PUT `/api/tasks/{id}`)**: a full edit, using optimis
          +tic concurrency.                                              
      113 +```json                                                       
      114 +{ "title": "…", "description": "…", "status": "InProgress", "p
          +riority": "High", "assignedTo": "Bob", "rowVersion": "AAAAAAAA
          +B9E=" }                                                       
      115 +```                                                           
      116 +- `title`, `status`, `priority` and `rowVersion` are required.
          + It's a full replacement, so an omitted `description`         
      117 +  or `assignedTo` is **cleared**.                             
      118 +- `rowVersion` must be the value from the last response for th
          +is task. If the task changed since, the response is           
      119 +  **409 Conflict** and nothing is saved. Reload and retry.    
      120 +- A missing `rowVersion` returns 400 `errors.rowVersion` ("Row
          +Version is required."). A malformed or wrong-length one       
      121 +  returns 400 ("RowVersion must be the base64 value returned b
          +y the API.").                                                 
      122 +- `id`, `createdDate`, `modifiedDate` and `isDeleted` can't be
          + changed, and are ignored if sent.                            
      123 +                                                              
      124 +**Change status (PATCH `/api/tasks/{id}/status`)**: body `{ "s
          +tatus": "Done" }`.                                            
      125 +- Last write wins, so no `rowVersion` is needed. Setting the s
          +tatus the task already has changes nothing.                   
      126 +- The response carries the task's **new** `rowVersion`. Client
          +s must keep it, because a later PUT with the old one gets 409.
      127 +                                                              
      128 +**Dates:** `modifiedDate` is set to the current UTC time on ev
          +ery write. `createdDate` never changes.

  Full suite: Passed!  - Failed: 0, Passed: 167, Skipped: 0, Total: 167
  The most important one is PutAfterPatch_…. After a PATCH, a PUT that still
  carries the old rowVersion gets 409. With the rowVersion the PATCH returned,
  it gets 200. That's why the UI must replace its copy of a task with every
  response.

  How to verify

  dotnet build
  dotnet test
  dotnet run --project src/TaskManager.Api --launch-profile http
  Then use the new "update" and "change status" sections of
  src/TaskManager.Api/TaskManager.Api.http, in order:
  1. # @name task1 GET /api/tasks/1 captures the current rowVersion.
  2. PUT /api/tasks/1 uses {{task1.response.body.$.rowVersion}}. Expect 200, the
     new values, a new rowVersion, a later modifiedDate and the same
     createdDate.
  3. Run the same PUT again. Its rowVersion is now stale, so expect 409 
     Conflict.
  4. PUT with no rowVersion → 400 errors.rowVersion "RowVersion is required.".
     With "bad" → 400 "RowVersion must be the base64 value returned by the
     API.".
  5. PUT /api/tasks/13 (seeded, soft-deleted) → 404.
  6. PATCH /api/tasks/2/status {"status":"Done"} → 200 with a new rowVersion.
     Sending it again → 200 with nothing changed, same rowVersion and
     modifiedDate.
  7. PATCH with "Blocked" → 400 errors.status. PATCH on 13 → 404.

  The {{task1…}} variable works in VS Code's REST Client, Rider, and Visual
  Studio 17.12 or later. These requests change rows 1 and 2 in your dev
  database.

  Things you should know

  - Shared request fields: CreateTaskRequest now inherits title, description,
    priority and assignedTo from a new TaskFieldsRequest base class. All of the
    existing create tests still pass, which shows its behaviour didn't change.
  - Locked build: the first PUT build failed because your running API had the
    executable locked. The suite only counted as passing after you stopped it
    and I rebuilt.
  - PATCH race: PATCH can still return 409 in one rare case, when another write
    lands between loading the task and saving it. That's the only conflict left
    under last-write-wins.

● Approved Claude's plan
  ⎿  Plan saved to: 
     ~\.claude\plans\pasted-content-id-0656-i-m-working-expressive-umbrella.md ·
     /plan to edit
     Step 7: Soft delete (DELETE /api/tasks/{id})

     Context

     Step 6 is committed (5d741d7): PUT (with optimistic concurrency) and PATCH
     status, both built on
     TaskService.FindTrackedAsync, which loads through the soft-delete global
     query filter, so unknown and
     deleted ids both throw NotFoundException → 404. Step 7 adds DELETE 
     /api/tasks/{id}. It marks the task
     IsDeleted = true and never removes the row. The query filter then hides it
     from GET, the list, PUT, PATCH and
     (in step 8) the summary.

     Behaviour

     - Success → 204 No Content, with no body.
     - Unknown or already-deleted id → 404 ProblemDetails ("Task with id '5' was
       not found."). The
       FindTrackedAsync path is reused, so deleting twice gives 404 the second
       time. /api/tasks/0 → 404 via the
       {id:int:min(1)} route constraint.
     - Dates (confirmed): ModifiedDate = TimeProvider.GetUtcNow() on delete, and
       no DeletedDate column.
       Deleting is a write, so ModifiedDate should record it. A deleted task
       can't be edited afterwards, so its
       ModifiedDate is the deletion time. This avoids a migration for a field
       the brief doesn't list. CreatedDate never changes.
     - Concurrency (confirmed): last-write-wins, with no rowVersion, like PATCH
       status.
       - DELETE requests normally carry no body.
       - It's a soft delete, so nothing is lost if it races with an edit: the
         row and its latest values stay in the
         table and can be restored by hand.
       - The rare write that lands between load and save still surfaces as 409,
         through the existing handler.
     - Logging: Soft-deleted task {TaskId} at Information. CORS already allows
       DELETE.

     Code

     - ITaskService.DeleteAsync(int id, CancellationToken) (XML-documented: 404
       on unknown or deleted).
     - TaskService.DeleteAsync: var task = await FindTrackedAsync(id, ct); 
       task.IsDeleted = true; task.ModifiedDate = _timeProvider.GetUtcNow(); 
       await _db.SaveChangesAsync(ct); then log.
       Nothing else changes: Title, Status and the rest are kept, and so is
       CreatedDate.
     - TasksController.Delete(int id, ct): [HttpDelete("{id:int:min(1)}")],
       [ProducesResponseType(204)],
       [ProducesResponseType<ProblemDetails>(404, "application/problem+json")],
       returns NoContent().

         at T0, advance the clock 3h, delete, then reload with
         IgnoreQueryFilters and assert IsDeleted, Modified = T0+3h, Created =
         T0, and the other fields unchanged.
       - DeleteAsync_ThenGetById_ThrowsNotFound

         ModifiedDate later than before, CreatedDate

         list that matched it, while other tasks remain
       - delete twice → 204 then 404
       - unknown id → 404 with the detail message
       - /api/tasks/0 → 404
       - PUT and PATCH on a deleted task → 404

● Step 7 is done: dotnet test passes all 180 tests (13 new). The .http file and

  DeleteAsync in TaskService.cs

  /// <inheritdoc />
  public async Task DeleteAsync(int id, CancellationToken cancellationToken)
  {
      // Unknown and already-deleted ids both fail here (global query filter), 
  so a second delete is 404.
      var task = await FindTrackedAsync(id, cancellationToken);

      // Soft delete: the row stays; the global query filter hides it from every
  query from now on.
      // ModifiedDate records when it was deleted (a deleted task can't be 
  edited afterwards).
      task.IsDeleted = true;
      task.ModifiedDate = _timeProvider.GetUtcNow();

      await _db.SaveChangesAsync(cancellationToken);

      _logger.LogInformation("Soft-deleted task {TaskId}", task.Id);
  }
  The controller action calls it and returns NoContent() (204). Unknown and
  already-deleted ids go through the existing NotFoundException → 404 path.

  (2026-01-06 14:00 +00:00)

  back, run UPDATE TaskManagerDb.dbo.Tasks SET IsDeleted = 0 WHERE Id = 5;. Its
  ModifiedDate will still show the deletion time.

     ~\.claude\plans\pasted-content-id-0656-i-m-working-expressive-umbrella.md ·
     /plan to edit
     Step 8: GET /api/tasks/summary (raw SQL via ADO.NET)

     Context

     Step 7 is committed (79ba01e). The brief requires a summary endpoint built
     on raw SQL, not EF: task counts
     grouped by status and priority. Decisions already made: plain ADO.NET
     (Microsoft.Data.SqlClient, which comes
     transitively from the EF SqlServer package, so no new package), and a flat
     list [{ status, priority, count }]
     containing only combinations that have tasks, with [] when there are none.

     Contract

     - Contracts/TaskSummaryItem.cs: record TaskSummaryItem(TaskItemStatus 
       Status, TaskPriority Priority, int Count).
       The existing JsonStringEnumConverter serializes it as
       {"status":"Todo","priority":"High","count":2}.

     Service (Services/ITaskSummaryService.cs, Services/TaskSummaryService.cs)

     - ITaskSummaryService.GetSummaryAsync(CancellationToken) →
       IReadOnlyList<TaskSummaryItem>. Registered as scoped in
       AddTaskServices().
     - TaskSummaryService(IOptions<DatabaseSettings>, 
       ILogger<TaskSummaryService>). The connection string comes from the
       same validated ConnectionStrings:DefaultConnection via DatabaseSettings.
       It doesn't touch the DbContext.
     - The SQL is a public const string SummarySql. It has no concatenation and
       no parameters, because there's no user input.
       Any future filters would go through typed SqlParameters only.
     SELECT [Status], [Priority], COUNT(*) AS [Count]
     FROM [dbo].[Tasks]
     WHERE [IsDeleted] = 0            -- the EF global query filter does NOT 
     apply to raw SQL
     GROUP BY [Status], [Priority]
     ORDER BY
         CASE [Status]   WHEN N'Todo' THEN 1 WHEN N'InProgress' THEN 2 WHEN
     N'Done' THEN 3 ELSE 4 END,
         CASE [Priority] WHEN N'Critical' THEN 1 WHEN N'High' THEN 2 WHEN
     N'Medium' THEN 3 WHEN N'Low' THEN 4 ELSE 5 END;
     - Execution:
       - await using SqlConnection, then OpenAsync(ct)
       - await using SqlCommand(SummarySql, connection), then
         ExecuteReaderAsync(ct)
       - loop on ReadAsync(ct), reading columns by ordinal (GetOrdinal once,
         then GetString / GetString / GetInt32)
       - log Summary returned {Rows} rows at Debug level
       - SQL errors bubble up to GlobalExceptionHandler, which returns a 500
         with no details
     - Mapping and enum parsing: see the decision below.

     Route

     [HttpGet("summary")] on TasksController, which gets ITaskSummaryService
     injected next to ITaskService.
     There are two separate reasons it can't collide with GET /api/tasks/{id}:
     - the id route is constrained to {id:int:min(1)}, and "summary" isn't an
       int
     - attribute routing ranks literal segments above parameter segments anyway

     A test calls /api/tasks/summary and expects 200 with an array, not 404. The
     existing GET-by-id tests keep passing.

     Decisions (confirmed: parse into enums; action lives in TasksController)

     Enum parsing. The SQL returns the strings stored by EF's
     HasConversion<string>().
     - The service parses them into the enums with Enum.Parse<TEnum>(value),
       exact case, which matches EF's stored names.
     - That keeps the contract typed and consistent with TaskResponse.
     - The CK_Tasks_Status and CK_Tasks_Priority check constraints guarantee
       valid names. If a bad value somehow got in,
       it would fail loudly as a 500 with a logged error, not be passed silently
       to the UI.
     - The alternative is plain string fields in the DTO: no parsing, but no
       type safety either.

     Tests

     - Unit (Unit/TaskSummarySqlTests.cs), no database:
       - SummarySql contains WHERE [IsDeleted] = 0
       - every TaskItemStatus and TaskPriority name appears in the CASE
         expressions. This is a drift guard: adding an enum
         value without updating the ORDER BY fails the test.
       - the SQL contains no { or + concatenation markers. That one is cheap and
         mostly documents intent.
     - Integration (Integration/Tasks/SummaryTests.cs, Database collection),
       using this fixture of active rows:
       - Todo/High ×2, Todo/Medium ×1, Todo/Low ×1
       - InProgress/Critical ×1
       - Done/Critical ×3
       - plus soft-deleted rows: Todo/High ×1, which must not raise its count to
         3, and Done/Low ×1, a combo that only
         has deleted rows and so must be absent

       The tests:
       - exact list and order: Todo/High 2, Todo/Medium 1, Todo/Low 1,
         InProgress/Critical 1, Done/Critical 3.
         Alphabetical order would put Done first and Low before Medium, so this
         proves the CASE ordering.
       - combinations with no tasks are absent (count of 5 items; Done/Low not
         present)
       - soft-deleted rows are excluded
       - empty DB → 200 []; only-deleted rows → []
       - JSON shape: "status" and "priority" are strings and "count" is a number
       - the filter really matters: run SummarySql with WHERE [IsDeleted] = 0
         removed (asserting the replace actually
         changed the text) directly through ADO.NET against the same fixture. It
         returns Todo/High = 3 and includes Done/Low.
         The real endpoint returns 2 and no Done/Low. This proves the fixture
         would catch a missing filter.
       - the route resolves: /api/tasks/summary → 200 array, not a 404 from the
         id route

● Update(README.md)  ⎿  Added 21 lines
      128  - Last write wins, so no `rowVersion` is needed. Setting the s
           tatus the task already has changes nothing.
      129  - The response carries the task's **new** `rowVersion`. Client
           s must keep it, because a later PUT with the old one gets 409.
      130 
      131 +**Summary (GET `/api/tasks/summary`)**: counts of active tasks
          + grouped by status and priority.                              
      132 +```json                                                       
      133 +[ { "status": "Todo", "priority": "High", "count": 2 }, { "sta
          +tus": "Done", "priority": "Critical", "count": 3 } ]          
      134 +```                                                           
      135 +- Only combinations that have tasks are returned. Soft-deleted
          + tasks are not counted.                                       
      136 +- Order: status Todo, InProgress, Done, then priority Critical
          +, High, Medium, Low.                                          
      137 +- Implemented with **raw SQL via plain ADO.NET** (`Services/Ta
          +skSummaryService.cs`), not EF Core. It uses the same          
      138 +  `ConnectionStrings:DefaultConnection`. The SQL is a constant
          + with no user input:                                          
      139 +  ```sql                                                      
      140 +  SELECT [Status], [Priority], COUNT(*) AS [Count]            
      141 +  FROM [dbo].[Tasks]                                          
      142 +  WHERE [IsDeleted] = 0                                       
      143 +  GROUP BY [Status], [Priority]                               
      144 +  ORDER BY                                                    
      145 +      CASE [Status] WHEN N'Todo' THEN 1 WHEN N'InProgress' THE
          +N 2 WHEN N'Done' THEN 3 ELSE 4 END,                           
      146 +      CASE [Priority] WHEN N'Critical' THEN 1 WHEN N'High' THE
          +N 2 WHEN N'Medium' THEN 3 WHEN N'Low' THEN 4 ELSE 5 END;      
      147 +  ```                                                         
      148 +  `WHERE [IsDeleted] = 0` is required because EF's soft-delete
          + query filter doesn't apply to raw SQL. The CASE              
      149 +  ranks are needed because the enums are stored as strings, wh
          +ich would otherwise sort alphabetically. Any future           
      150 +  filter must be added as a typed `SqlParameter`, never by con
          +catenating text.                                              
      151 +                                                              
      152  **Delete (DELETE `/api/tasks/{id}`)**: a soft delete. The row
           is kept with `IsDeleted = 1` and from then on is
      153  hidden from every endpoint (GET, list, PUT, PATCH, summary). D
           eleting again returns 404. Last write wins, so no
      154  `rowVersion` is needed. There is no `DeletedDate` column: `mod
           ifiedDate` records the deletion time, because a

● Step 8 is done: dotnet test passes all 193 tests (13 new), and nothing is
  committed. Below are the ADO.NET code, the SQL and the requests to try by
  hand.

  TaskSummaryService.cs

  public sealed class TaskSummaryService : ITaskSummaryService
  {
      /// The summary query. A constant: no concatenation and no user input, so 
  no parameters are needed;
      /// any future filter must be added as a typed SqlParameter.
      /// WHERE [IsDeleted] = 0 is required because EF Core's global soft-delete
  filter does not apply to raw SQL.
      /// The ORDER BY uses CASE ranks because the enums are stored as strings.
      public const string SummarySql = """
          SELECT [Status], [Priority], COUNT(*) AS [Count]
          FROM [dbo].[Tasks]
          WHERE [IsDeleted] = 0
          GROUP BY [Status], [Priority]
          ORDER BY
              CASE [Status] WHEN N'Todo' THEN 1 WHEN N'InProgress' THEN 2 WHEN 
  N'Done' THEN 3 ELSE 4 END,
              CASE [Priority] WHEN N'Critical' THEN 1 WHEN N'High' THEN 2 WHEN 
  N'Medium' THEN 3 WHEN N'Low' THEN 4 ELSE 5 END;
          """;

      private readonly string _connectionString;
      private readonly ILogger<TaskSummaryService> _logger;

      public TaskSummaryService(IOptions<DatabaseSettings> databaseSettings,
  ILogger<TaskSummaryService> logger)
      {
          _connectionString = databaseSettings.Value.ConnectionString;   // same
  validated ConnectionStrings:DefaultConnection
          _logger = logger;
      }

      public async Task<IReadOnlyList<TaskSummaryItem>>
  GetSummaryAsync(CancellationToken cancellationToken)
      {
          await using var connection = new SqlConnection(_connectionString);
          await connection.OpenAsync(cancellationToken);

          await using var command = new SqlCommand(SummarySql, connection);
          await using var reader = await
  command.ExecuteReaderAsync(cancellationToken);

          var statusOrdinal = reader.GetOrdinal("Status");
          var priorityOrdinal = reader.GetOrdinal("Priority");
          var countOrdinal = reader.GetOrdinal("Count");

          var items = new List<TaskSummaryItem>();
          while (await reader.ReadAsync(cancellationToken))
          {
              // Values are the exact enum names written by EF 
  (HasConversion<string>) and guarded by the
              // CK_Tasks_Status / CK_Tasks_Priority check constraints, so an 
  exact-case parse is safe.
              // An unexpected value would throw and surface as a logged 500 
  rather than bad data.
              items.Add(new TaskSummaryItem(
                  Enum.Parse<TaskItemStatus>(reader.GetString(statusOrdinal)),
                  Enum.Parse<TaskPriority>(reader.GetString(priorityOrdinal)),
                  reader.GetInt32(countOrdinal)));
          }

          _logger.LogDebug("Task summary returned {Rows} rows", items.Count);
          return items;
      }
  }
  (XML doc comments trimmed here.)
  - Disposal: the connection, command and reader are all await using, so they're
    released even if the query fails.
  - Cancellation: the CancellationToken is passed to all three async calls.
  - The endpoint: it's [HttpGet("summary")] on TasksController. It can't clash
    with /api/tasks/{id}, because that route only matches an int ≥ 1, and a test
    checks both routes still work.

● Approved Claude's plan

     ~\.claude\plans\pasted-content-id-0656-i-m-working-expressive-umbrella.md ·
     /plan to edit
     Step 9: React UI scaffold, API layer, task list with filters

     Context

     The API is complete and committed (steps 1–8). Step 9 starts the UI in
     src/task-manager-ui: a Vite + React app
     in plain CSS with an /api layer that parses our ProblemDetails once, plus
     the task list with status and priority
     filters. Detail, create/edit, delete, status dropdown and summary views
     come in steps 10–11. CLAUDE.md rules apply:
     functional components, hooks, one component per file, and API calls only in
     src/api.

     Tooling and versions (Node v26.13.1, npm 11.20.0)

     Node 26 is fine for all of these. Every package's engines range accepts it,
     and vitest and jsdom list >=26
     explicitly. The real risk is how new the latest releases are: vite 8.3.8,
     vitest 5.1.1, jsdom 31.0.1, user-event
     14.6.13, and jest-dom 8.0.0, a new major released yesterday. So I'll pin
     exact versions that are at least two weeks
     old (published before 2026-11-06), set save-exact=true in .npmrc, and
     commit package-lock.json.

     ┌─────────────────────────────┬───────────────────┬────────────┐
     │           Package           │      Pinned       │ Published  │
     ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
     │ react / react-dom           │ 19.3.1            │ 2026-10-14 │
     ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
     │ vite                        │ 8.3.6             │ 2026-11-05 │
     ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
     │ @vitejs/plugin-react        │ 6.2.1             │ 2026-11-02 │
     ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
     │ vitest                      │ 5.1.0             │ 2026-11-02 │
     ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
     │ jsdom                       │ 31.0.0            │ 2026-11-05 │
     ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
     │ @testing-library/react      │ 16.3.3            │ 2026-08-27 │
     ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
     │ @testing-library/dom        │ 10.4.2            │ 2026-09-13 │
     ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
     │ @testing-library/jest-dom   │ 7.0.1 (not 8.0.0) │ 2026-08-10 │
     ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
     │ @testing-library/user-event │ 14.6.8            │ 2026-10-13 │
     └─────────────────────────────┴───────────────────┴────────────┘

     - I'll hand-write the scaffold rather than run npm create vite, so the
       template doesn't pull unpinned ^latest
       versions or bring in ESLint. package.json gets "engines": { "node": 
       ">=22.12" }.
     - vite.config.js sets server: { port: 5173, strictPort: true }. If 5173 is
       busy, Vite fails instead of moving to
       5174, which the API's CORS policy would reject.

     Decisions (all confirmed by you)

     1. JavaScript or TypeScript: JavaScript, with JSDoc @typedefs for the API
        shapes in src/api.
        - TS would type-check the contract (TaskResponse, status and priority
          unions) and catch prop and field typos at build
          time. That costs a tsconfig, @types/* packages, .tsx files and a tsc
          step.
        - For about 10 small components with tests on the API layer, JS plus
          JSDoc gives editor hints and stays smaller.
     2. API base URL: VITE_API_BASE_URL, with no Vite proxy.
        - .env.development holds VITE_API_BASE_URL=http://localhost:5157. It's
          committed because it's not a secret. A
          .env.development.local override is gitignored.
        - The browser calls the API directly, so we exercise the CORS policy we
          built and tested. That's also how a separately
          hosted UI would behave in production.
        - A proxy would hide CORS problems in dev. If the variable is missing,
          the API layer raises a clear configuration error.
     3. List layout: a table on wide screens that stacks into cards below about 
        640px, using CSS only.
        - A table is the most scannable for 12+ rows with five fields: title,
          status, priority, assignee, modified.
        - Stacking keeps it usable on a phone.

     API layer (src/api/)

     - config.js: reads import.meta.env.VITE_API_BASE_URL and trims a trailing
       slash.
     - ApiError.js (class): status, title, detail, fieldErrors ({ 
       [camelCaseKey]: string[] }), traceId, and
       kind ('http', 'network' or 'config').
     - httpClient.js: request(path, { method, query, body, signal }). It:
       - builds the URL with URLSearchParams and skips empty or undefined query 
         values
       - sends JSON with Accept: application/json
       - on a non-OK response, parses application/problem+json once into an
         ApiError. Field keys stay camelCase, and
         JSON-path keys such as $.priority are normalised to priority ($ becomes
         _form). A non-JSON error body gets a
         generic title for its status.
       - on a fetch TypeError (API down or CORS blocked), throws
         ApiError{kind:'network', title:"Can't reach the API at <url>. Is it 
         running?"}
       - lets AbortError through unchanged, so hooks can ignore cancelled
         requests
       - returns null for 204
     - tasksApi.js: listTasks({status, priority}, {signal}), getTask(id),
       createTask(body), updateTask(id, body),
       updateTaskStatus(id, status), deleteTask(id) and getSummary(). The whole
       API surface is done now so steps 10–11
       only add UI. Each one is a single request call.
     - JSDoc typedefs: Task, TaskSummaryItem, TaskFilters.

     UI (src/)

     - constants/taskOptions.js: STATUSES and PRIORITIES (value, label, icon),
       shared by filters and badges.
     - hooks/useTasks.js: useTasks(filters) returns { tasks, loading, error, 
       reload }. It re-fetches whenever the filters
       change, uses an AbortController so a slow earlier response can't
       overwrite a newer one, and keeps the previous rows
       while refreshing.
     - Components, one per file, each with a co-located CSS file where it needs
       one:
       - App.jsx: the header and the filter state { status: '', priority: '' }
       - TaskFilters.jsx: two labelled <select>s ("All statuses", Todo, In
         progress, Done / "All priorities", Critical…Low)
         plus a "Clear filters" button. It calls onChange, never the API.
       - TaskList.jsx: table or cards, a row count, and an aria-busy loading
         state
       - TaskRow.jsx: one row: title, description preview, status, priority,
         assignee ("Unassigned"), modified date (Intl.DateTimeFormat)
       - PriorityBadge.jsx: colour plus a text label plus an icon, so it isn't
         colour-only. Critical ▲▲ red, High ▲ orange,
         Medium ● amber, Low ▼ grey-blue. The icons are aria-hidden; the text
         label carries the meaning.
         Colours are chosen to meet WCAG AA contrast against the badge
         background.
       - StatusBadge.jsx: a neutral pill with its label
       - EmptyState.jsx: "No tasks match these filters" with a Clear filters
         button, or "No tasks yet" when no filters are set
       - ErrorBanner.jsx: role="alert", showing the title and detail plus a
         Retry button. The network error explains how to start the API.
     - Filters → API: a change to filter state makes useTasks call
       listTasks({ status, priority }) → GET 
       /api/tasks?status=Done&priority=High, leaving out empty values.
     - States:
       - first load shows a "Loading tasks…" placeholder
       - a refetch dims the table with aria-busy
       - an error shows the banner with Retry
       - an empty result shows the empty state
     - Styling: index.css holds CSS variables (neutral greys, one accent, an 8px
       spacing scale, system font stack,
       focus-visible rings), giving a clean, quiet, professional look with no
       framework.

     Tests (Vitest 5 + RTL, jsdom; fetch stubbed with vi.stubGlobal)

     - api/httpClient.test.js:
       - base URL and path joining
       - query building: empty and undefined values are dropped, values are
         encoded
       - the JSON body and headers are sent
       - 400 ValidationProblemDetails → ApiError with title, fieldErrors.title,
         and $.priority normalised to priority
       - 404 → title and detail
       - a non-JSON 500 → generic title
       - fetch TypeError → kind:'network'
       - AbortError is passed through
       - 204 → null
     - api/tasksApi.test.js: each function's method, URL and body.
       listTasks({status:'Done', priority:''}) →
       /api/tasks?status=Done.
     - components/TaskFilters.test.jsx: selecting a status or priority calls
       onChange with the right object, and Clear resets it.
     - App.test.jsx (with tasksApi mocked):
       - the initial load calls listTasks({status:'', priority:''})
       - choosing Done, then High, calls it with {status:'Done'} and then
         {status:'Done', priority:'High'}
       - the loading, empty and error states render, and Retry re-fetches
     - components/PriorityBadge.test.jsx: each priority renders its text label.
       That's the not-colour-only check.

     Files (all new under src/task-manager-ui/)

     - package.json, package-lock.json, .npmrc, vite.config.js, index.html
     - .env.development, .gitignore (.env*.local)
     - src/main.jsx, src/index.css, and src/test/setup.js
     - the api/, constants/, hooks/ and components/ files listed above
     - README.md: the root README gets a UI section

     Verification

     cd src/task-manager-ui
     npm ci          # exact versions from package-lock.json
     npm test        # vitest run
     npm run build   # production build compiles
     # terminal 1 (repo root): dotnet run --project src/TaskManager.Api 
     --launch-profile http
     # terminal 2: npm run dev  → open http://localhost:5173
     - the list shows the active tasks, Critical first
     - the Status and Priority filters change the request: watch DevTools →
       Network for /api/tasks?status=…
     - a filter with no matches shows the empty state, and Clear filters resets
       it
     - stopping the API and pressing Retry shows the "Can't reach the API"
       banner
     - dotnet test stays green, since there are no API changes

● Update(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 36 lines, removed 3 lines
       2 
       3  A small task management app: a .NET 9 Web API (EF Core Code Fir
          st, SQL Server) and a React + Vite UI.
       4 
       5 -> Status: **step 8 of 11**: the API is complete. That covers th
         -e scaffold, cross-cutting concerns, data model and             
       6 -> seed data, plus the list, get, create, update (PUT), change-s
         -tatus (PATCH), soft-delete and summary endpoints.              
       7 -> The UI comes in later steps.                                 
       5 +> Status: **step 9 of 11**: the API is complete. The UI has its
         + scaffold, API layer, and a task list with status              
       6 +> and priority filters. Detail, create/edit, delete and summary
         + screens come in the next steps.                               
       7 
       8 +## Running the UI against the API                              
       9 +Use two terminals:                                             
      10 +```powershell                                                  
      11 +# 1. API (repo root)                                           
      12 +dotnet run --project src/TaskManager.Api --launch-profile http 
         +     # http://localhost:5157                                   
      13 +                                                               
      14 +# 2. UI                                                        
      15 +cd src/task-manager-ui                                         
      16 +npm ci                                                         
         +     # first time / after pulling                              
      17 +npm run dev                                                    
         +     # http://localhost:5173                                   
      18 +```                                                            
      19 +- The UI calls the API directly at `VITE_API_BASE_URL` (`src/ta
         +sk-manager-ui/.env.development`, default                       
      20 +  `http://localhost:5157`). There is no Vite proxy. The API's C
         +ORS policy allows `http://localhost:5173`, and the             
      21 +  dev server is pinned to that port (`strictPort`), so it fails
         + rather than silently moving to a port CORS would reject.      
      22 +- To point the UI at a different API, create `src/task-manager-
         +ui/.env.development.local` (gitignored) containing             
      23 +  `VITE_API_BASE_URL=http://host:port`. If the UI must run on a
         +nother origin, add it to the API's `Cors:AllowedOrigins`.      
      24 +- UI commands: `npm test` (Vitest, single run), `npm run test:w
         +atch`, `npm run build` (output in `dist/`), `npm run preview`. 
      25 +                                                               
      26 +### UI structure                                               
      27 +```                                                            
      28 +src/task-manager-ui/src/                                       
      29 +  api/          httpClient.js (the only fetch; parses ProblemDe
         +tails into ApiError), tasksApi.js, config.js, ApiError.js      
      30 +  hooks/        useTasks.js (loads the list, re-fetches on filt
         +er change, aborts stale requests)                              
      31 +  components/   TaskFilters, TaskList, TaskRow, PriorityBadge, 
         +StatusBadge, EmptyState, ErrorBanner (one per file, own CSS)   
      32 +  constants/    taskOptions.js (status/priority values, labels,
         + icons)                                                        
      33 +```                                                            
      34 +- **Errors:** every failure reaches components as an `ApiError`
         + with `title`, `detail`, camelCase `fieldErrors`               
      35 +  (`$.priority` is normalised to `priority`), `traceId`, and a 
         +`kind` of `http`, `network` (API not running or                
      36 +  blocked by CORS) or `config`.                                
      37 +- **Priority** is shown with colour plus an icon and a text lab
         +el (▲▲ Critical, ▲ High, ● Medium, ▼ Low), so it               
      38 +  never relies on colour alone.                                
      39 +- **Versions** are pinned exactly (`.npmrc` has `save-exact=tru
         +e`, and `package-lock.json` is committed), using               
      40 +  releases that were at least two weeks old when added.        
      41 +                                                               
      42  ## Prerequisites
      43  - .NET SDK 9.0.x (`global.json` pins to the 9.0 line and rolls
          forward to the latest installed 9.0 feature band)
      44  - SQL Server Express at `localhost\SQLEXPRESS` (from step 2 onw
          ards)

  Running the UI against the API

  # Terminal 1, repo root
  dotnet run --project src/TaskManager.Api --launch-profile http     # 
  http://localhost:5157

  # Terminal 2
  cd src/task-manager-ui
  npm ci            # only needed the first time
  npm run dev       # http://localhost:5173
  Open http://localhost:5173 and check:
  - The list: it shows your active tasks with Critical first. Each priority
    badge has an icon and a text label as well as colour.
  - The filters: changing Status or Priority calls GET 
    /api/tasks?status=…&priority=…. Watch DevTools → Network.
  - Empty state: a combination with no tasks shows "No tasks match these
    filters", and its Clear filters button resets the list.
  - Error state: stop the API and change a filter. The red banner should read
    "Can't reach the API at http://localhost:5157." Start the API again and
    press Retry.
  - Small screens: narrow the window below 640px and the table rows should stack
    into cards.

  The UI calls the API directly using VITE_API_BASE_URL from .env.development,
  with no proxy. The dev server only runs on port 5173, the one origin the API's
  CORS policy allows, and it refuses to start if that port is taken.

  Files to review

  All new files are under src/task-manager-ui/. node_modules/ and dist/ are
  gitignored.
  - Config:
    - package.json, package-lock.json and .npmrc (save-exact=true)
    - vite.config.js, index.html
    - .env.development (only the localhost API URL, no secrets) and .gitignore
